text(await tools.apply_patch("*** Begin Patch\n*** Add File: C:\\Users\\Joshu\\dev\\ai-os-projects\\lsatu-abstract\\src\\topics\\abstract\\components\\views\\RoleChoiceLecture.tsx\n+import { ArrowDown } from \"lucide-react\";\n+import { ANSWER_CHOICE_ITEMS } from \"../../data/answerChoiceDrill\";\n+\n+// Local lecture draft from ai-os/brainstorms/2026-10-13-abstract-answer-choice-review.md,\n+// \"Current lecture copy ready for local review\" (2026-10-13). Not publication approval.\n+// Source choice 2 and the example retain Desktop/role_question_answer_choices_complete_transcription.html.\n+// Connective reasoning is grounded in corpus/Curriculum/Role/Homework/_scan_Role_Questions_Recap_Drill.md,\n+// corpus/Curriculum/Abstract/Homework/Level 1 Answer Parsing and Mapping.md, and\n+// corpus/Curriculum/Abstract/Homework/Advanced Parsing Answer Key.md. Closing uses source choices 8–15.\n+const SOURCE_EXAMPLE_HTML = '<p class=\"example\"><span class=\"underlined\">John has been smoking two packs a day since he was sixteen, last week he was diagnosed with lung cancer.</span> Smoking is a direct cause of lung cancer. We must avoid cigarettes at all costs if we want to stay healthy.</p>';\n+\n+const PROSE = \"my-10 space-y-5 text-[19px] leading-[1.7] [&_strong]:font-bold\";\n+const HEADING = \"text-2xl md:text-3xl font-black leading-tight text-brutal-ink\";\n+\n+export function RoleChoiceGuide() {\n+  return (\n+    <section className={PROSE} aria-labelledby=\"role-choice-relationship\">\n+      <h2 id=\"role-choice-relationship\" className={HEADING}>An answer choice describes a relationship</h2>\n+      <p>An abstract answer choice tells us what a statement does in an argument. To understand it, we need more than the name of a role. We need to see the relationship the choice describes.</p>\n+      <p>A premise is a reason for accepting a conclusion. An example can illustrate that premise. Those are related jobs, but they are different jobs. The answer choice can ask us to follow both.</p>\n+      <p>Consider this original choice:</p>\n+      <blockquote className=\"border-l-4 border-brutal-ink bg-brutal-paper p-5 md:p-6 font-semibold italic\">\n+        {ANSWER_CHOICE_ITEMS[1].stemText}\n+      </blockquote>\n+      <p><strong>Illustration</strong> means an example. <strong>Premise</strong> tells us that the idea being illustrated is a reason the author uses. <strong>Support the argument’s conclusion</strong> tells us what that reason is used for.</p>\n+      <p>So there are two relationships to check: the statement must be an example of the premise, and the premise must support the conclusion. Calling the statement “an example” gets us started. It does not finish the work.</p>\n+\n+      <h3 className={`${HEADING} pt-6`}>Follow the original example</h3>\n+      <p>The following is the original source example, unchanged. The underlined statement is the one the choice describes:</p>\n+      <div\n+        className=\"border-2 border-brutal-ink bg-brutal-yellow/25 p-5 md:p-6 [&_.example]:m-0 [&_.example]:italic [&_.underlined]:underline [&_.underlined]:decoration-2 [&_.underlined]:underline-offset-4\"\n+        data-testid=\"guided-role-source-example\"\n+        dangerouslySetInnerHTML={{ __html: SOURCE_EXAMPLE_HTML }}\n+      />\n+      <p>First, find the point the author wants us to accept. Here, the author says we must avoid cigarettes if we want to stay healthy.</p>\n+      <p>What reason does the author give? <strong>Smoking is a direct cause of lung cancer.</strong> That is the premise used to support the recommendation.</p>\n+      <p>Now look at the underlined statement. John smoked two packs a day and was diagnosed with lung cancer. The source presents John’s case as an example of the causal relationship stated in the premise.</p>\n+      <p>We can now follow the whole description. <strong>John’s case illustrates the premise. The premise supports the conclusion.</strong> The choice describes what the underlined statement does, including the connection to the reason and the reason’s connection to the final point.</p>\n+\n+      <figure className=\"my-8 rounded-lg border-2 border-brutal-ink bg-brutal-paper p-5 md:p-7\" aria-label=\"John’s case illustrates the premise, which supports the conclusion\">\n+        <div className=\"rounded-lg border-2 border-brutal-ink bg-brutal-yellow/30 p-4 md:p-5\">\n+          <p className=\"mb-2 text-sm font-bold uppercase tracking-wider\">The example</p>\n+          <p className=\"underline decoration-2 underline-offset-4\">John has been smoking two packs a day since he was sixteen, last week he was diagnosed with lung cancer.</p>\n+        </div>\n+        <div className=\"flex flex-col items-center py-3 font-bold text-brutal-ink\">\n+          <span>illustrates</span><ArrowDown className=\"mt-1 h-6 w-6\" aria-hidden=\"true\" />\n+        </div>\n+        <div className=\"rounded-lg border-2 border-brutal-ink bg-brutal-paper p-4 md:p-5\">\n+          <p className=\"mb-2 text-sm font-bold uppercase tracking-wider\">The premise</p>\n+          <p className=\"font-semibold\">Smoking is a direct cause of lung cancer.</p>\n+        </div>\n+        <div className=\"flex flex-col items-center py-3 font-bold text-brutal-ink\">\n+          <span>supports</span><ArrowDown className=\"mt-1 h-6 w-6\" aria-hidden=\"true\" />\n+        </div>\n+        <div className=\"rounded-lg border-2 border-brutal-ink bg-brutal-yellow/30 p-4 md:p-5\">\n+          <p className=\"mb-2 text-sm font-bold uppercase tracking-wider\">The conclusion</p>\n+          <p className=\"font-semibold\">We must avoid cigarettes at all costs if we want to stay healthy.</p>\n+        </div>\n+      </figure>\n+\n+      <p>If we only noticed that the paragraph discusses smoking, we would miss the structure. If we only noticed that John’s case is an example, we would leave part of the choice unchecked. We need to know what it is an example of and how that idea is used in the argument.</p>\n+      <p>This explains the source example’s role. It does not ask us to decide whether the argument has proved its causal claim. The task here is to understand the description and identify the relationship the source is showing.</p>\n+    </section>\n+  );\n+}\n+\n+export function RoleChoicePracticeInstructions() {\n+  return (\n+    <section className={PROSE} aria-labelledby=\"role-choice-concrete\">\n+      <h2 id=\"role-choice-concrete\" className={HEADING}>Make each choice concrete</h2>\n+      <p>For each of the fifteen choices, there are two pieces of written work you can use to test your understanding.</p>\n+      <div className=\"space-y-5 rounded-lg border-2 border-brutal-ink bg-brutal-paper p-5 md:p-6\">\n+        <p><strong>State the requirements.</strong> What would have to appear in an argument for the choice to describe it correctly? Keep every part of the description. A claim receiving support is different from a claim receiving no justification. A claim being required is different from a claim establishing the conclusion.</p>\n+        <p><strong>Create an example argument.</strong> Write reasons and a conclusion, then identify the particular statement the choice would describe. Check where each requirement appears in your example. If one part is missing, you know what still needs work.</p>\n+      </div>\n+      <p>The original breakdown is available whenever you need it. You can try the written work first, open the explanation to learn the relationship, or return to revise your example after reading. Reading the breakdown and completing the written work are recorded separately.</p>\n+      <p>As you continue, keep comparing the wording of the choice with what the statement actually does. The explanation should make the language more concrete. Your example gives you a chance to use that meaning yourself.</p>\n+    </section>\n+  );\n+}\n+\n+export function RoleChoiceClosing() {\n+  return (\n+    <section className={PROSE} aria-labelledby=\"role-choice-whole-description\">\n+      <h2 id=\"role-choice-whole-description\" className={HEADING}>Check the whole description</h2>\n+      <p>The fifteen choices use different words, but the task stays the same: turn the description into requirements you can check.</p>\n+      <p>Start with the idea the choice names. Is it an example, a principle, an observation, a hypothesis, or a conclusion? Then look at what happens to that idea. Is it used as support, challenged, denied, or offered as an explanation?</p>\n+      <p>Keep the strength of the wording. <strong>Required</strong> and <strong>establishes</strong> do different work. <strong>Attempts</strong> does not tell us that the attempt succeeds. <strong>Explicitly stated</strong> asks for something the argument actually says. Leaving out one of these details changes the description you are trying to satisfy.</p>\n+      <p>The final choices also show why we trace each link of support. A statement can receive support and then support the main conclusion. Or it can be the main conclusion, supported by another statement that itself receives support. Ask why we should believe each claim and look for the reason the argument gives. Then ask what that claim supports in turn.</p>\n+      <p>You can return to any choice and check your own example against its original breakdown. Have you included every required part? The four Desktop introduction paragraphs and all fifteen breakdowns remain exact. The following four lessons are **newly authored review drafts**, grounded in existing source content. This task edits this review artifact only, not app code or memory. No implementation, approval or publication is claimed for these new drafts. The initial M7 source gap was resolved by the bounded model-library route: three complete original questions and keys were verified in the captured local database. Its snapshot is dated 2026-08-03; this is source evidence, not a current live-backend check.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Module 4 draft: match every requirement to the argument\\r\\n\\r\\nPurpose: move from describing what an answer needs to pointing to the actual parts of one full source argument. Unlike Module 3’s learner-created examples, this lesson supplies the argument and asks the learner to locate the relationship.\\r\\n\\r\\n#### A description has to fit this argument\\r\\n\\r\\nAn answer choice can sound familiar and still describe the wrong relationship. Now we’ll use an argument that is already written. For every requirement in the choice, we need to point to the part of that argument that fulfills it.\\r\\n\\r\\nRead this original exchange:\\r\\n\\r\\n<p class=\\"example\\"><strong>John:</strong> <span class=\\"underlined\\">Celebrities have the biggest smiles in front of the camera</span>, so they must be the happiest people alive.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p class=\\"example\\"><strong>David:</strong> That is not true. Many celebrities suffer from depression and substance abuse. Smiling in front of the camera doesn’t mean a thing, they could simply be putting on a show for the audience.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\nThe statement we are examining is John’s observation that celebrities have the biggest smiles in front of the camera. David is responding to John’s argument. Keep those two speakers separate as we read the choice.\\r\\n\\r\\nHere is the original answer choice:\\r\\n\\r\\n> It makes an observation that, according to the argument, is insufficient to justify the claim that the argument concludes is false\\r\\n\\r\\n#### Find the claim the author rejects\\r\\n\\r\\nThe choice says that the argument concludes a claim is false. Which claim?\\r\\n\\r\\nJohn says celebrities must be the happiest people alive. David opens with “That is not true,” then points to depression and substance abuse. So the rejected claim is John’s conclusion about their happiness. The statement under examination is not David’s objection. It is the observation John used to reach the claim David rejects.\\r\\n\\r\\n#### Find the observation and check whether it is enough\\r\\n\\r\\nJohn’s reason is the smiles he sees in front of the camera. David says smiling “doesn’t mean a thing” and suggests celebrities could be putting on a show. David is challenging what those smiles establish. They do not justify John’s conclusion, according to David’s argument.\\r\\n\\r\\nWe can now match the description completely:\\r\\n\\r\\n| Requirement in the choice | Where it appears in the original argument |\\r\\n|---|---|\\r\\n| An observation is made | John: celebrities have the biggest smiles in front of the camera |\\r\\n| The observation is used to justify a claim | John uses the smiles to conclude celebrities are the happiest people alive |\\r\\n| The author treats the observation as insufficient | David says the smiles do not establish happiness and could be a performance |\\r\\n| The argument concludes the supported claim is false | David rejects John’s claim and cites depression and substance abuse |\\r\\n\\r\\n**False describes the claim David rejects. It does not describe the observation about the smiles.** That difference matters. If we moved “false” onto the observation, we would be reading a different choice.\\r\\n\\r\\nBefore accepting a description, say which statement it refers to and point to every relationship it requires. A passage containing all the right topics is not enough. The right statements must be doing the right jobs.\\r\\n\\r\\nPractice instruction draft: reread the original exchange and choice. Identify the observation, the claim it supports, and David’s response in separate written lines. Compare each line with the matching table. This is a source comparison, not an invented multiple-choice question.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Module 5 draft: see the actual move, not just the topic\\r\\n\\r\\nPurpose: identify how the author uses a reason to challenge a conclusion. This adds an actual worked method question, rather than a methods-label list.\\r\\n\\r\\n#### Follow the rule to the case the economist chooses\\r\\n\\r\\nThe argument below is about taxation. That is its subject. The question asks how the economist responds to the politician, so we need to follow what the economist does with the politician’s reasoning.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Original question PT103-S1-Q18**\\r\\n\\r\\nPolitician: A government that taxes incomes at a rate of 100 percent will generate no revenue because all economic activity will cease. So it follows that the lower the rate of income tax, the more revenue the government will generate by that tax.\\r\\n\\r\\nEconomist: Your conclusion cannot be correct, since it would mean that an income tax of 0 percent would generate the maximum revenue.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Original question stem:** Which one of the following argumentative strategies is used by the economist in responding to the politician?\\r\\n\\r\\n**A.** stating a general principle that is incompatible with the conclusion the politician derives\\r\\n\\r\\n**B.** providing evidence that where the politician’s advice has been adopted, the results have been disappointing\\r\\n\\r\\n**C.** arguing that the principle derived by the politician, if applied in the limiting case, leads to an absurdly false conclusion\\r\\n\\r\\n**D.** undermining the credibility of the politician by openly questioning the politician’s understanding of economics\\r\\n\\r\\n**E.** attacking the politician’s argument by giving reason to doubt the truth of a premise\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\nStart with the politician’s conclusion: the lower the income-tax rate, the more revenue the tax generates. The politician takes the result at a 100-percent rate and draws a broad relationship between lower rates and higher revenue.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe economist does not cite a country where a tax cut failed. She does not dispute that economic activity would cease at the 100-percent rate. She takes the politician’s conclusion to the other end of the range: a rate of zero percent.\\r\\n\\r\\nIf lowering the rate always raises revenue, a zero-percent rate would produce the maximum. But a tax charged at zero percent collects no income-tax revenue. The economist uses that consequence to challenge the politician’s conclusion.\\r\\n\\r\\n**That is the reasoning move: apply the politician’s rule at its lower limit and show that the resulting conclusion cannot be right.**\\r\\n\\r\\n#### Connect that move to the answer’s language\\r\\n\\r\\nChoice C calls the politician’s conclusion a **principle**. Here it is the general relationship the politician claims: lower rate, more revenue. **If applied in the limiting case** refers to applying that relationship at the zero-percent rate. **Leads to an absurdly false conclusion** refers to the claim that a zero-percent income tax would generate maximum revenue.\\r\\n\\r\\nEach part names something we can point to. The answer is C in the captured source key.\\r\\n\\r\\nNotice how other choices change the action. B describes real results from places that adopted the advice; no such evidence is given. D describes an open attack on the politician’s understanding of economics; the economist instead points to a consequence of the politician’s conclusion. E says a premise is challenged; the response challenges the conclusion drawn from the premise.\\r\\n\\r\\nBefore selecting a methods label, explain the actual move in ordinary words. Here, “the economist uses a zero-percent tax to show what goes wrong with the politician’s rule” connects the reason to the response. “They disagree about taxes” does not.\\r\\n\\r\\nPractice draft: identify the politician’s rule, the case the economist applies it to, and the result that makes the rule untenable. Then match those three parts to C. This is a worked source question; no new seventeen-category bank is added.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Module 6 draft: a possible explanation is not a proven explanation\\r\\n\\r\\nPurpose: diagnose the precise word or relationship that makes a close description go beyond the argument.\\r\\n\\r\\n#### Keep the author’s degree of commitment\\r\\n\\r\\nA description can correctly identify the topic and still say the author did more than the passage shows. Read this full original question. Pay attention to **unwarranted**, **might instead**, and **might then** in the stimulus.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Original question PT139-S1-Q23**\\r\\n\\r\\nAnthropologist: Every human culture has taboos against eating certain animals. Some researchers have argued that such taboos originated solely for practical reasons, pointing out, for example, that in many cultures it is taboo to eat domestic animals that provide labor and that are therefore worth more alive than dead. But that conclusion is unwarranted; taboos against eating certain animals might instead have arisen for symbolic, ritualistic reasons, and the presence of the taboos might then have led people to find other uses for those animals.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Original question stem:** In the argument, the anthropologist\\r\\n\\r\\n**A.** calls an explanation of a phenomenon into question by pointing out that observations cited as evidence supporting it are also compatible with an alternative explanation of the phenomenon\\r\\n\\r\\n**B.** establishes that an explanation of a phenomenon is false by demonstrating that the evidence that had been cited in support of that explanation was inadequate\\r\\n\\r\\n**C.** rejects the reasoning used to justify a hypothesis about the origins of a phenomenon, on the grounds that there exists another, more plausible hypothesis about the origins of that phenomenon\\r\\n\\r\\n**D.** argues in support of one explanation of a phenomenon by citing evidence incompatible with a rival explanation\\r\\n\\r\\n**E.** describes a hypothesis about the sequence of events involved in the origins of a phenomenon, and then argues that those events occurred in a different sequence\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\nThe researchers conclude that the taboos originated solely for practical reasons. They point to useful domestic animals that are worth more alive than dead.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe anthropologist says that conclusion is unwarranted. Why? There is another possible explanation: symbolic or ritualistic taboos could have come first, and people could then have found practical uses for the protected animals.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe observation about useful animals fits that alternative too. So the anthropologist challenges whether the cited observation settles the origin of the taboo. The argument does not establish which origin actually occurred.\\r\\n\\r\\n#### Check the correct description completely\\r\\n\\r\\nChoice A says the author **calls an explanation into question**. That fits “that conclusion is unwarranted.” It then says the observations cited as evidence are **also compatible with an alternative explanation**. That fits the possibility that taboos came first and practical uses were found afterward.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe source key is A. Both parts match. The alternative can account for the observation without contradicting the fact that those animals have practical value.\\r\\n\\r\\n#### Find the exact point where each nearby choice changes the argument\\r\\n\\r\\nB says the author **establishes ... is false**. The author challenges whether the researchers have established their explanation; she does not establish that it is false. “Unwarranted” does not do the same work as “false.”\\r\\n\\r\\nC calls the alternative **more plausible**. The author raises it as a possibility. She does not compare the explanations and establish that hers is more plausible.\\r\\n\\r\\nD describes evidence **incompatible with a rival explanation**. The point is that the same observation can fit another explanation. Compatibility is the reason the original explanation has not been settled.\\r\\n\\r\\nE says the author **argues that those events occurred in a different sequence**. The source presents another sequence with “might.” It does not conclude that sequence actually occurred.\\r\\n\\r\\nThis is how we diagnose a close answer. Quote the word that adds strength or changes the relationship, then compare it with what the author actually commits to. Do not replace a careful comparison with “it sounds too strong.” Explain what is too strong and why.\\r\\n\\r\\nPractice draft: for B through E, identify the exact phrase that does not fit and point to the stimulus wording that settles the mismatch. Then explain how both parts of A fit. All five choices are original source text, not manufactured distractors.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Module 7 draft: put the whole procedure to work on a fresh question\\r\\n\\r\\nPurpose: combine structure, decoding, full requirement mapping, and precise rejection of alternatives. This full question is outside Module 3’s fifteen choices and outside the worked examples in Modules 4–6.\\r\\n\\r\\n#### Make your own account before opening the explanation\\r\\n\\r\\nRead the original argument and all five choices. Identify the columnist’s conclusion and the reason for it. Explain the author’s move in ordinary language. Then choose a description and show where each part of it appears in the argument.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Original question PT118-S4-Q12**\\r\\n\\r\\nColumnist: If you received an unsigned letter, you would likely have some doubts about the truth of its contents. But news stories often include statements from anonymous sources, and these are usually quoted with the utmost respect. It makes sense to be skeptical of these sources, for, as in the case of the writer of an unsigned letter, their anonymity makes it possible for them to plant inaccurate or slanted statements without ever having to answer for them.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Original question stem:** The columnist\'s argument proceeds by\\r\\n\\r\\n**A.** pointing out that a certain attitude would presumably be adopted in one situation, in order to support the claim that a similar attitude would be justified in an analogous situation\\r\\n\\r\\n**B.** drawing an analogy between an attitude commonly adopted in one situation and a different attitude commonly adopted in another situation, and establishing that the latter attitude is better justified than the former\\r\\n\\r\\n**C.** inferring that an attitude would be justified in all situations of a given type on the grounds that this attitude is justified in a hypothetical situation of that type\\r\\n\\r\\n**D.** calling into question a certain type of evidence by drawing an analogy between that evidence and other evidence that the argument shows is usually false\\r\\n\\r\\n**E.** calling into question the motives of those presenting certain information, and concluding for this reason that the information is likely to be false\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n#### First, follow the argument\\r\\n\\r\\nThe conclusion is that it makes sense to be skeptical of anonymous sources in news stories. The columnist compares those sources with the writer of an unsigned letter.\\r\\n\\r\\nWhat connects the cases? In both, anonymity allows inaccurate or slanted statements without the speaker or writer having to answer for them. The columnist uses the doubt we would likely have about an unsigned letter to support skepticism toward anonymous news sources.\\r\\n\\r\\nThat gives us an account of the move before we rely on a label: **use our likely response to one anonymous source to justify a similar response to another, where the same anonymity problem applies**.\\r\\n\\r\\n#### Decode the full answer and map every part\\r\\n\\r\\nThe captured source key is A. Break its description into requirements:\\r\\n\\r\\n| Part of A | Match in the original argument |\\r\\n|---|---|\\r\\n| A certain attitude would presumably be adopted | You would likely have doubts about the truth of an unsigned letter |\\r\\n| In one situation | Receiving an unsigned letter |\\r\\n| To support the claim that a similar attitude would be justified | The columnist concludes skepticism makes sense |\\r\\n| In an analogous situation | Reading news stories that include anonymous sources |\\r\\n\\r\\nThe final sentence explains why the situations are compared: in each, anonymity allows inaccurate or slanted claims without accountability. The analogy is doing support work, rather than merely mentioning two different kinds of writing.\\r\\n\\r\\n#### Check the alternatives without changing the conclusion\\r\\n\\r\\nB says the **latter attitude is better justified**. That would defend the respect given to anonymous sources over the doubt directed toward unsigned letters. The conclusion instead supports skepticism toward anonymous sources.\\r\\n\\r\\nC says the hypothetical case is **of that type**. The hypothetical is receiving an unsigned letter; the conclusion concerns anonymous sources in news stories. They are compared as analogous situations, rather than treated as the same type of situation.\\r\\n\\r\\nD says the analogous evidence is **usually false**. The source says we would likely have doubts about an unsigned letter. Doubts about truth do not establish that such letters are usually false.\\r\\n\\r\\nE says the conclusion is that the information is **likely to be false**. The columnist concludes that skepticism makes sense. The possibility of inaccurate or slanted statements supports that cautious attitude; it does not establish the stronger conclusion E states.\\r\\n\\r\\n#### Explain your choice fully\\r\\n\\r\\nIf your answer matched A, check whether you matched every part or only recognized “analogous.” If you chose another answer, identify the exact phrase that changed the attitude, the situation, or the strength of the conclusion. Then revise your written account of the argument.\\r\\n\\r\\nYou have now used the whole procedure on a fresh full source question: understand the structure, decode the description, list its requirements, match them to the argument, and check each qualification. The same procedure gives you a reason to accept the answer and a precise reason to reject a close alternative.\\r\\n\\r\\nThis completes the course’s applied sequence. You can return to the fifteen-choice exercise for language practice or to these source questions to practice mapping that language to an argument.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Source map and builder contract for Modules 4–7\\r\\n\\r\\n| Module | Exact reused source | New authored material | Availability/status |\\r\\n|---|---|---|---|\\r\\n| M4 | Desktop Role item 4: full John/David exchange and exact choice | Mapping explanation/table and written source-comparison task | Draft ready for review; source full argument available |\\r\\n| M5 | Full original PT103-S1-Q18 stimulus, prompt, five choices; source key C | Work through limiting-case objection and map full C | Draft ready for review; no 17-method bank added |\\r\\n| M6 | Full original PT139-S1-Q23 stimulus, prompt, five choices; source key A | Diagnose exact strength/compatibility/sequence traps in B–E | Draft ready for review; all distractors original |\\r\\n| M7 | Full original PT118-S4-Q12 stimulus, prompt, five choices; source key A | Full-procedure analogy map and exact wrong-choice diagnosis | Fresh full source question verified locally; no M3 duplicate |\\r\\n\\r\\nSupporting sources: [matching strategy](../raw/sources/lsat-u/curriculum/Abstract/_source/03-art-of-matching-keywords.md) for structure before keyword matching and noun/action/qualification checks; [Role recap](../corpus/Curriculum/Role/Homework/_scan_Role_Questions_Recap_Drill.md) for support and trap diagnosis; [Advanced Parsing key](../corpus/Curriculum/Abstract/Homework/Advanced%20Parsing%20Answer%20Key.md) for the fresh original miniargument; Exact question IDs: PT103-S1-Q18 (`034a6efb-1779-4ce7-94da-32fe77125c79`, key C); PT139-S1-Q23 (`629dd4c6-9bff-47b0-8cb9-ff42e15810ac`, key A); PT118-S4-Q12 (`335729e1-a638-4dde-ab58-160af1332859`, key A). No publication, account-acceptance or mastery claim follows from this draft.\\r\\n\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\n# Current lecture copy ready for local review\\r\\n\\r\\nDate: 2026-10-13. **Draft connective teaching for Joshua review.** This is the current builder-ready proposal. It supersedes the guided choice-1 proposal and conditional lecture ending farther below. The lesson uses source choice 2 because its example shows the example-to-premise-to-conclusion relationship directly. No new scenarios are invented.\\r\\n\\r\\nPlacement: keep the Desktop source’s four introduction paragraphs exactly; follow them with the opening and guided example below. Keep the fifteen original choices/breakdowns exact and in order. Place the closing after the fifteenth original breakdown. No additional required modules are proposed. Source introduction/breakdown HTML remains untouched.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Draft opening and guided source example\\r\\n\\r\\n### An answer choice describes a relationship\\r\\n\\r\\nAn abstract answer choice tells us what a statement does in an argument. To understand it, we need more than the name of a role. We need to see the relationship the choice describes.\\r\\n\\r\\nA premise is a reason for accepting a conclusion. An example can illustrate that premise. Those are related jobs, but they are different jobs. The answer choice can ask us to follow both.\\r\\n\\r\\nConsider this choice:\\r\\n\\r\\n> It is an illustration of a premise that is used to support the argument’s conclusion\\r\\n\\r\\n**Illustration** means an example. **Premise** tells us that the idea being illustrated is a reason the author uses. **Support the argument’s conclusion** tells us what that reason is used for.\\r\\n\\r\\nSo there are two relationships to check: the statement must be an example of the premise, and the premise must support the conclusion. Calling the statement “an example” gets us started. It does not finish the work.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Follow the example\\r\\n\\r\\nThe underlined statement is the one the choice describes.\\r\\n\\r\\n<p class=\\"example\\"><span class=\\"underlined\\">John has been smoking two packs a day since he was sixteen, last week he was diagnosed with lung cancer.</span> Smoking is a direct cause of lung cancer. We must avoid cigarettes at all costs if we want to stay healthy.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\nFirst, find the point the author wants us to accept. Here, the author says we must avoid cigarettes if we want to stay healthy.\\r\\n\\r\\nWhat reason does the author give? **Smoking is a direct cause of lung cancer.** That is the premise used to support the recommendation.\\r\\n\\r\\nNow look at the underlined statement. John smoked two packs a day and was diagnosed with lung cancer. John’s case is an example of the causal relationship stated in the premise.\\r\\n\\r\\nWe can now follow the whole description. **John’s case illustrates the premise. The premise supports the conclusion.** The choice describes what the underlined statement does, including the connection to the reason and the reason’s connection to the final point.\\r\\n\\r\\nIf we only noticed that the paragraph discusses smoking, we would miss the structure. If we only noticed that John’s case is an example, we would leave part of the choice unchecked. We need to know what it is an example of and how that idea is used in the argument.\\r\\n\\r\\nThis explains the statement’s role. It does not ask us to decide whether the argument has proved its causal claim. The task here is to understand the description and identify the relationship the source is showing.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Make each choice concrete\\r\\n\\r\\nFor each of the fifteen choices, there are two pieces of written work you can use to test your understanding.\\r\\n\\r\\n**State the requirements.** What would have to appear in an argument for the choice to describe it correctly? Keep every part of the description. A claim receiving support is different from a claim receiving no justification. A claim being required is different from a claim establishing the conclusion.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Create an example argument.** Write reasons and a conclusion, then identify the particular statement the choice would describe. Check where each requirement appears in your example. If one part is missing, you know what still needs work.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe original breakdown is available whenever you need it. You can try the written work first, open the explanation to learn the relationship, or return to revise your example after reading. Reading the breakdown and completing the written work are recorded separately.\\r\\n\\r\\nAs you continue, keep comparing the wording of the choice with what the statement actually does. The explanation should make the language more concrete. Your example gives you a chance to use that meaning yourself.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Draft closing after the fifteenth exact breakdown\\r\\n\\r\\n### Check the whole description\\r\\n\\r\\nThe fifteen choices use different words, but the task stays the same: turn the description into requirements you can check.\\r\\n\\r\\nStart with the idea the choice names. Is it an example, a principle, an observation, a hypothesis, or a conclusion? Then look at what happens to that idea. Is it used as support, challenged, denied, or offered as an explanation?\\r\\n\\r\\nKeep the strength of the wording. **Required** and **establishes** do different work. **Attempts** does not tell us that the attempt succeeds. **Explicitly stated** asks for something the argument actually says. Leaving out one of these details changes the description you are trying to satisfy.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe final choices also show why we trace each link of support. A statement can receive support and then support the main conclusion. Or it can be the main conclusion, supported by another statement that itself receives support. Ask why we should believe each claim and look for the reason the argument gives. Then ask what that claim supports in turn.\\r\\n\\r\\nYou can return to any choice and check your own example against its original breakdown. Have you included every required part? Have you identified the statement the choice describes? Does that statement actually do what the full choice says?\\r\\n\\r\\nThat completes the fifteen-choice exercise. Next, we’ll use a source argument to match each requirement to the statement and relationship it describes.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Source map for this ready copy\\r\\n\\r\\n- Desktop `role_question_answer_choices_complete_transcription.html`: exact choice 2 and its complete example paragraph; breakdown 2 identifies smoking/causation as the premise and avoiding cigarettes as the conclusion. This draft adds reasoning connections without changing that example or the original breakdown.\\r\\n- Desktop choices/breakdowns 8–15: unsupported versus supported claim, required versus establishes, explicit conclusion, and support chains. The closing’s distinctions come from these original sections.\\r\\n- [Role recap drill](../corpus/Curriculum/Role/Homework/_scan_Role_Questions_Recap_Drill.md), Level 4: asks why we should believe a claim and whether the argument supplies a supporting statement. Its overbroad “only conclusion” claim is not adopted.\\r\\n- [Level 1 Answer Parsing and Mapping](../corpus/Curriculum/Abstract/Homework/Level%201%20Answer%20Parsing%20and%20Mapping.md), Part III: reverse-engineer requirements and create an example. No extra worksheet questions are imported.\\r\\n- [Advanced Parsing Answer Key](../corpus/Curriculum/Abstract/Homework/Advanced%20Parsing%20Answer%20Key.md), supported premise: a claim receives evidence and supports the main conclusion. Its separate methods/flaw material is outside this lecture.\\r\\n- [Teaching voice](../wiki/teaching-voice.md), [selected teaching standard](../references/lsat-teaching-voice-standard.md), [copy corrections](../wiki/COPY-CORRECTIONS.md): plain meaning before terminology, visible reasoning, familiar source example, complete qualifications. Original supplied wording remains exact.\\r\\n\\r\\nEverything outside the quoted choice and original example is newly authored draft language. List what the stimulus must contain.</strong> One item per piece.\\r\\n      </p>\\r\\n      <p>\\r\\n        <strong>3. Check the strong words.</strong> One word too strong sinks the choice.\\r\\n      </p>\\r\\n      <p>\\r\\n        The quiz gives you ten answer choices you haven\'t decoded yet. The drill gives you four full Flaw\\r\\n        questions. Run the routine on every choice, not just the first one that sounds right.\\r\\n      </p>\\r\\n    </div>\\r\\n  );\\r\\n}\\r\\n\\r\\nexport const NOTE_COMPONENTS: Record<string, () => JSX.Element> = {\\r\\n  why_hard: WhyHardNote,\\r\\n  words_to_know: WordsNote,\\r\\n  deabstractifying_method: BreakItDownNote,\\r\\n  art_of_matching: MatchingNote,\\r\\n  master_keys: MethodNote,\\r\\n  five_bucket_indicators: TrapsNote,\\r\\n  abstract_15: PutTogetherNote,\\r\\n};\\r\\n"}'}]

text(await tools.exec_command({cmd:"$draftText = [IO.File]::ReadAllText('C:\\Users\\Joshu\\dev\\ai-os\\brainstorms\\2026-10-13-abstract-answer-choice-review.md')\n$moduleDrafts = @{}\nforeach ($number in 4..7) {\n  $nextBoundary = if ($number -lt 7) { '### Module ' + ($number + 1) + ' draft:' } else { '### Source map and builder contract' }\n  $pattern = '### Module ' + $number + ' draft:[\\s\\S]*?(?=' + [regex]::Escape($nextBoundary) + ')'\n  $section = [regex]::Match($draftText, $pattern).Value\n  $start = $section.IndexOf('#### ')\n  if ($start -lt 0) { throw \"Missing module $number draft\" }\n  $moduleDrafts[$number] = $section.Substring($start).Trim()\n}\nfunction FormatInline([string] $text) {\n  $text = [System.Net.WebUtility]::HtmlEncode($text)\n  $text = [regex]::Replace($text, '\\*\\*(.+?)\\*\\*', '<strong>$1</strong>')\n  return $text\n}\nfunction FormatLesson([string] $text) {\n  $blocks = [regex]::Split($text.Trim(), '\\r?\\n\\s*\\r?\\n')\n  $html = [Collections.Generic.List[string]]::new()\n  foreach ($block in $blocks) {\n    $block = $block.Trim()\n    if (-not $block) { continue }\n    if ($block -match '^\\*\\*Original question PT') { continue }\n    $block = $block.Replace('**Original question stem:** ', '')\n    if ($block.StartsWith('<p ')) { $html.Add($block); continue }\n    if ($block.StartsWith('#### ')) { $html.Add('<h2>' + (FormatInline $block.Substring(5)) + '</h2>'); continue }\n    if ($block.StartsWith('> ')) { $html.Add('<blockquote>' + (FormatInline $block.Substring(2)) + '</blockquote>'); continue }\n    if ($block.StartsWith('|')) {\n      $rows = [regex]::Split($block, '\\r?\\n')\n      $table = '<div class=\"overflow-x-auto\"><table><thead><tr>'\n      foreach ($cell in $rows[0].Trim('|').Split('|')) { $table += '<th>' + (FormatInline $cell.Trim()) + '</th>' }\n      $table += '</tr></thead><tbody>'\n      foreach ($row in $rows | Select-Object -Skip 2) { $table += '<tr>'; foreach ($cell in $row.Trim('|').Split('|')) { $table += '<td>' + (FormatInline $cell.Trim()) + '</td>' }; $table += '</tr>' }\n      $html.Add($table + '</tbody></table></div>'); continue\n    }\n    if ($block.StartsWith('**') -and $block.EndsWith('**') -and [regex]::Matches($block, '\\*\\*').Count -eq 2) { $html.Add('<div class=\"definition\"><p>' + (FormatInline $block) + '</p></div>'); continue }\n    $html.Add('<p>' + (FormatInline $block) + '</p>')\n  }\n  return $html -join \"`n\"\n}\n$moduleDrafts[4] = $moduleDrafts[4].Replace('Practice instruction draft: ', '').Replace('This is a source comparison, not an invented multiple-choice question.', '').Replace('Read this original exchange:', 'Read this exchange:').Replace('Here is the original answer choice:', 'Here is the answer choice:')\n$moduleDrafts[5] = $moduleDrafts[5].Replace('She does not dispute', 'The economist does not dispute').Replace('She takes', 'The economist takes').Replace('The answer is C in the captured source key.', 'The answer is C.').Replace('Practice draft: ', '').Replace('This is a worked source question; no new seventeen-category bank is added.', '')\n$moduleDrafts[6] = $moduleDrafts[6].Replace('The source key is A.', 'The answer is A.').Replace('she does not establish', 'the anthropologist does not establish').Replace('that hers is more plausible', 'that the alternative is more plausible').Replace('The source presents another sequence', 'The anthropologist presents another sequence').Replace('Practice draft: ', '').Replace('All five choices are original source text, not manufactured distractors.', '')\n$moduleDrafts[7] = $moduleDrafts[7].Replace('The captured source key is A.', 'Answer A describes the move.').Replace('The source says we would likely', 'The columnist says we would likely')\n$explanationStarts = @{5='Start with the politician’s conclusion:'; 6='The researchers conclude that'; 7='#### First, follow the argument'}\n$constants = 'const MATCHING_HTML = ' + (ConvertTo-Json -InputObject (FormatLesson $moduleDrafts[4]) -Compress) + \";`n\"\nforeach ($number in 5..7) {\n  $splitAt = $moduleDrafts[$number].IndexOf($explanationStarts[$number])\n  if ($splitAt -lt 0) { throw \"Missing explanation boundary module $number\" }\n  $intro = FormatLesson $moduleDrafts[$number].Substring(0, $splitAt)\n  $explanation = FormatLesson $moduleDrafts[$number].Substring($splitAt)\n  $constants += 'const MODULE_' + $number + '_INTRO = ' + (ConvertTo-Json -InputObject $intro -Compress) + \";`n\"\n  $constants += 'const MODULE_' + $number + '_EXPLANATION = ' + (ConvertTo-Json -InputObject $explanation -Compress) + \";`n\"\n}\n$componentTop = @'\nimport { useState } from \"react\";\n\n// Local authored lessons from the top M4-M7 draft in ai-os/brainstorms/\n// 2026-10-13-abstract-answer-choice-review.md (2026-10-13); pending Joshua review.\n// M4: Desktop role_question_answer_choices_complete_transcription.html, item 4.\n// M5-M7: captured 2026-08-03 lr_questions.json/lr_answer_choices.json via the LR model index:\n// PT103-S1-Q18 (034a6efb-1779-4ce7-94da-32fe77125c79),\n// PT139-S1-Q23 (629dd4c6-9bff-47b0-8cb9-ff42e15810ac),\n// PT118-S4-Q12 (335729e1-a638-4dde-ab58-160af1332859).\n// Reasoning also grounded in Abstract 03-art-of-matching-keywords, Role recap,\n// Advanced Parsing Answer Key, and Method and Flaw Strategy Guide. No added scenarios.\n\n'@\n$componentBottom = @'\n\nfunction LessonProse({ html }: { html: string }) {\n  return (\n    <div\n      className=\"choice-breakdown-prose !px-0 text-[19px] leading-[1.8] [&_h2]:font-serif [&_h2]:text-[28px] [&_h2]:leading-tight [&_h2]:mt-10 [&_h2]:mb-5 [&_blockquote]:bg-brutal-yellow/30 [&_blockquote]:border-l-4 [&_blockquote]:border-brutal-ink [&_blockquote]:p-5 [&_blockquote]:italic [&_blockquote]:my-7 [&_strong]:font-bold [&_.underlined]:underline [&_.underlined]:underline-offset-4 [&_table]:w-full [&_table]:min-w-[460px] [&_table]:my-7 [&_table]:text-left [&_table]:text-base [&_th]:bg-brutal-yellow/30 [&_th]:p-4 [&_th]:border [&_th]:border-brutal-ink/20 [&_td]:p-4 [&_td]:border [&_td]:border-brutal-ink/20 [&_.definition_p]:m-0\"\n      dangerouslySetInnerHTML={{ __html: html }}\n    />\n  );\n}\n\nexport function RequirementMatchingLesson() {\n  return <section><LessonProse html={MATCHING_HTML} /></section>;\n}\n\nfunction WorkedLesson({ intro, explanation, revealLabel }: { intro: string; explanation: string; revealLabel: string }) {\n  const [revealed, setRevealed] = useState(false);\n  return (\n    <section className=\"space-y-7\">\n      <LessonProse html={intro} />\n      <button type=\"button\" className=\"brutal-btn bg-brutal-ink text-brutal-paper\" aria-expanded={revealed} onClick={() => setRevealed(!revealed)}>{revealed ? \"Hide the explanation\" : revealLabel}</button>\n      {revealed && <LessonProse html={explanation} />}\n    </section>\n  );\n}\n\nexport function ReasoningMoveLesson() {\n  return <WorkedLesson intro={MODULE_5_INTRO} explanation={MODULE_5_EXPLANATION} revealLabel=\"Follow the economist’s reasoning\" />;\n}\n\nexport function PreciseDescriptionLesson() {\n  return <WorkedLesson intro={MODULE_6_INTRO} explanation={MODULE_6_EXPLANATION} revealLabel=\"Compare the choices with the argument\" />;\n}\n\nexport function CompleteProcedureLesson() {\n  const [answer, setAnswer] = useState<string | null>(null);\n  const [account, setAccount] = useState(\"\");\n  const [revealed, setRevealed] = useState(false);\n  return (\n    <section className=\"space-y-7\">\n      <LessonProse html={MODULE_7_INTRO} />\n      <label className=\"block text-[19px] font-bold\" htmlFor=\"whole-procedure-account\">Explain the columnist’s move in ordinary words.</label>\n      <textarea id=\"whole-procedure-account\" rows={4} maxLength={5000} value={account} onChange={(event) => setAccount(event.target.value)} className=\"w-full rounded-lg border-2 border-brutal-ink bg-brutal-paper p-4 text-lg leading-relaxed\" />\n      <fieldset>\n        <legend className=\"mb-3 text-[19px] font-bold\">Which choice describes the move?</legend>\n        <div className=\"flex flex-wrap gap-3\">\n          {[\"A\", \"B\", \"C\", \"D\", \"E\"].map((letter) => <button key={letter} type=\"button\" aria-pressed={answer === letter} onClick={() => { setAnswer(letter); setRevealed(false); }} className={`brutal-btn ${answer === letter ? \"bg-brutal-ink text-brutal-paper\" : \"bg-brutal-paper\"}`}>Choice {letter}</button>)}\n        </div>\n      </fieldset>\n      <button type=\"button\" disabled={!answer} aria-expanded={revealed} onClick={() => setRevealed(!revealed)} className=\"brutal-btn bg-brutal-ink text-brutal-paper\">{revealed ? \"Hide the explanation\" : \"Compare my choice with the explanation\"}</button>\n      {revealed && <LessonProse html={MODULE_7_EXPLANATION} />}\n    </section>\n  );\n}\n'@\n$outPath = 'C:\\Users\\Joshu\\dev\\ai-os-projects\\lsatu-abstract\\src\\topics\\abstract\\components\\views\\AdvancedChoiceLessons.tsx'\n[IO.File]::WriteAllText($outPath, $componentTop + $constants + $componentBottom, [Text.UTF8Encoding]::new($false))","max_output_tokens":2000}))

continue }\n    if ($block.StartsWith('#### ')) { $html.Add('<h2>' + (FormatInline $block.Substring(5)) + '</h2>'); continue }\n    if ($block.StartsWith('> ')) { $html.Add('<blockquote>' + (FormatInline $block.Substring(2)) + '</blockquote>'); No added scenarios.\n\n'@\n$componentBottom = @'\n\nfunction LessonProse({ html }: { html: string }) {\n  return <div className=\"choice-breakdown-prose !px-0 [&_h2]:font-serif [&_h2]:text-[28px] explanation: string; revealLabel: string }) {\n  const [revealed, setRevealed] = useState(false);\n  return <section className=\"space-y-7\">\n    <LessonProse html={intro} />\n    <SourceQuestion [{'type': 'input_text', 'text': 'Script completed\nWall time 11.4 seconds\nOutput:\n'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"chunk_id":"06a110","wall_time_seconds":0.3869743,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":331,"output":"import type { DrillItem } from \\"../types\\";\\r\\n\\r\\n// Original PT snapshot questions. Source text/key captured in the approved local\\r\\n// review artifact (2026-10-13); explanations are source-grounded local drafts.\\r\\nexport const APPLIED_SOURCE_QUESTIONS: DrillItem[] = [\\r\\n  {\\r\\n    \\"id\\": \\"source_pt103_s1_q18\\",\\r\\n    \\"type\\": \\"method\\",\\r\\n    \\"stimulus\\": \\"Politician: A government that taxes incomes at a rate of 100 percent will generate no revenue because all economic activity will cease. So it follows that the lower the rate of income tax, the more revenue the government will generate by that tax.\\\\n\\\\nEconomist: Your conclusion cannot be correct, since it would mean that an income tax of 0 percent would generate the maximum revenue.\\",\\r\\n    \\"question\\": \\"Which one of the following argumentative strategies is used by the economist in responding to the politician?\\",\\r\\n    \\"options\\": [\\r\\n      {\\r\\n        \\"text\\": \\"stating a general principle that is incompatible with the conclusion the politician derives\\",\\r\\n        \\"explanation\\": \\"No incompatible general principle is stated.\\"\\r\\n      },\\r\\n      {\\r\\n        \\"text\\": \\"providing evidence that where the politician’s advice has been adopted, the results have been disappointing\\",\\r\\n        \\"explanation\\": \\"No results from places adopting the advice are cited.\\"\\r\\n      },\\r\\n      {\\r\\n"}'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"chunk_id":"6df975","wall_time_seconds":0.4432468,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":1958,"output":"4:// Local authored lessons from the top M4-M7 draft in ai-os/brainstorms/\\n7:// M5-M7: captured 2026-08-03 lr_questions.json/lr_answer_choices.json via the LR model index:\\n15:const MODULE_6_EXPLANATION = \\"<p>The researchers conclude that the taboos originated solely for practical reasons. They point to useful domestic animals that are worth more alive than dead.</p>\\\\n<p>The anthropologist says that conclusion is unwarranted. Why? There is another possible explanation: symbolic or ritualistic taboos could have come first, and people could then have found practical uses for the protected animals.</p>\\\\n<p>The observation about useful animals fits that alternative too. So the anthropologist challenges whether the cited observation settles the origin of the taboo. The argument does not establish which origin actually occurred.</p>\\\\n<h2>Check the correct description completely</h2>\\\\n<p>Choice A says the author <strong>calls an explanation into question</strong>. That fits “that conclusion is unwarranted.” It then says the observations cited as evidence are <strong>also compatible with an alternative explanation</strong>. That fits the possibility that taboos came first and practical uses were found afterward.</p>\\\\n<p>The answer is A. Both parts match. The alternative can account for the observation without contradicting the fact that those animals have practical value.</p>\\\\n<h2>Find the exact point where each nearby choice changes the argument</h2>\\\\n<p>B says the author <strong>establishes ... is false</strong>. The author challenges whether the researchers have established their explanation; the anthropologist does not establish that it is false. “Unwarranted” does not do the same work as “false.”</p>\\\\n<p>C calls the alternative <strong>more plausible</strong>. The author raises it as a possibility. She does not compare the explanations and establish that the alternative is more plausible.</p>\\\\n<p>D describes evidence <strong>incompatible with a rival explanation</strong>. The point is that the same observation can fit another explanation. Compatibility is the reason the original explanation has not been settled.</p>\\\\n<p>E says the author <strong>argues that those events occurred in a different sequence</strong>. The anthropologist presents another sequence with “might.” It does not conclude that sequence actually occurred.</p>\\\\n<p>This is how we diagnose a close answer. Quote the word that adds strength or changes the relationship, then compare it with what the author actually commits to. Do not replace a careful comparison with “it sounds too strong.” Explain what is too strong and why.</p>\\\\n<p>for B through E, identify the exact phrase that does not fit and point to the stimulus wording that settles the mismatch. Then explain how both parts of A fit.</p>\\";\\n17:const MODULE_7_EXPLANATION = \\"<h2>First, follow the argument</h2>\\\\n<p>The conclusion is that it makes sense to be skeptical of anonymous sources in news stories. The columnist compares those sources with the writer of an unsigned letter.</p>\\\\n<p>What connects the cases? In both, anonymity allows inaccurate or slanted statements without the speaker or writer having to answer for them. The columnist uses the doubt we would likely have about an unsigned letter to support skepticism toward anonymous news sources.</p>\\\\n<p>That gives us an account of the move before we rely on a label: <strong>use our likely response to one anonymous source to justify a similar response to another, where the same anonymity problem applies</strong>.</p>\\\\n<h2>Decode the full answer and map every part</h2>\\\\n<p>Answer A describes the move. Break its description into requirements:</p>\\\\n<div class=\\\\\\"overflow-x-auto\\\\\\"><table><thead><tr><th>Part of A</th><th>Match in the original argument</th></tr></thead><tbody><tr><td>A certain attitude would presumably be adopted</td><td>You would likely have doubts about the truth of an unsigned letter</td></tr><tr><td>In one situation</td><td>Receiving an unsigned letter</td></tr><tr><td>To support the claim that a similar attitude would be justified</td><td>The columnist concludes skepticism makes sense</td></tr><tr><td>In an analogous situation</td><td>Reading news stories that include anonymous sources</td></tr></tbody></table></div>\\\\n<p>The final sentence explains why the situations are compared: in each, anonymity allows inaccurate or slanted claims without accountability. The analogy is doing support work, rather than merely mentioning two different kinds of writing.</p>\\\\n<h2>Check the alternatives without changing the conclusion</h2>\\\\n<p>B says the <strong>latter attitude is better justified</strong>. That would defend the respect given to anonymous sources over the doubt directed toward unsigned letters. The conclusion instead supports skepticism toward anonymous sources.</p>\\\\n<p>C describes an inference about <strong>all situations of a given type</strong> from a hypothetical situation <strong>of that type</strong>. That is not the reasoning the columnist gives. The columnist uses the likely doubt about an unsigned letter to support a similar cautious attitude toward anonymous news sources, explaining the feature the cases share. The passage does not establish a universal rule for every situation in a defined category merely from one hypothetical case. Check the whole description of the inference: A captures the comparison and the justified similar attitude. Do not reject C just because the examples have different names; analogous cases can share a broader category. The mismatch is C’s description of a same-type general inference to all situations, rather than the analogy the author actually uses.</p>\\\\n<p>D says the analogous evidence is <strong>usually false</strong>. The columnist says we would likely have doubts about an unsigned letter. Doubts about truth do not establish that such letters are usually false.</p>\\\\n<p>E says the conclusion is that the information is <strong>likely to be false</strong>. The columnist concludes that skepticism makes sense. The possibility of inaccurate or slanted statements supports that cautious attitude; it does not establish the stronger conclusion E states.</p>\\\\n<h2>Explain your choice fully</h2>\\\\n<p>If your answer matched A, check whether you matched every part or only recognized “analogous.” If you chose another answer, identify the exact phrase that changed the attitude, the situation, or the strength of the conclusion. Then revise your written account of the argument.</p>\\\\n<p>You have now used the whole procedure on a fresh full source question: understand the structure, decode the description, list its requirements, match them to the argument, and check each qualification. The same procedure gives you a reason to accept the answer and a precise reason to reject a close alternative.</p>\\\\n<p>This completes the course’s applied sequence. You can return to the fifteen-choice exercise for language practice or to these source questions to practice mapping that language to an argument.</p>\\\\n<p>### Implementation checkpoint for the retained modules, 2026-10-13</p>\\\\n<p>Root accepted the four lessons above for local implementation. Notes/build agents are applying them and verifying the result. Original source introduction and fifteen breakdowns remain exact. The later lessons use one deliberate source-example revisit for M4 and complete sourced questions for M5–M7. The existing Final Set is held because its synthetic questions have defects; it is not treated as accepted source practice or released. No completed build, account acceptance, publication or parity claim is made in this checkpoint. Older course-ending-at-M3 proposals and implementation snapshots below are historical and superseded by Joshua’s explicit keep-Modules-4–7 decision.</p>\\";\\n"}'}]

Now we’ll use an argument that is already written. For every requirement in the choice, we need to point to the part of that argument that fulfills it.</p>\\\\n<p>Read this exchange:</p>\\\\n<p class=\\\\\\"example\\\\\\"><strong>John:</strong> <span class=\\\\\\"underlined\\\\\\">Celebrities have the biggest smiles in front of the camera</span>, so they must be the happiest people alive.</p>\\\\n<p class=\\\\\\"example\\\\\\"><strong>David:</strong> That is not true. Many celebrities suffer from depression and substance abuse. Smiling in front of the camera doesn’t mean a thing, they could simply be putting on a show for the audience.</p>\\\\n<p>The statement we are examining is John’s observation that celebrities have the biggest smiles in front of the camera. David is responding to John’s argument. Keep those two speakers separate as we read the choice.</p>\\\\n<p>Here is the answer choice:</p>\\\\n<blockquote>It makes an observation that, according to the argument, is insufficient to justify the claim that the argument concludes is false</blockquote>\\\\n<h2>Find the claim the author rejects</h2>\\\\n<p>The choice says that the argument concludes a claim is false. Which claim?</p>\\\\n<p>John says celebrities must be the happiest people alive. David opens with “That is not true,” then points to depression and substance abuse. So the rejected claim is John’s conclusion about their happiness. The statement under examination is not David’s objection. It is the observation John used to reach the claim David rejects.</p>\\\\n<h2>Find the observation and check whether it is enough</h2>\\\\n<p>John’s reason is the smiles he sees in front of the camera. David says smiling “doesn’t mean a thing” and suggests celebrities could be putting on a show. David is challenging what those smiles establish. They do not justify John’s conclusion, according to David’s argument.</p>\\\\n<p>We can now match the description completely:</p>\\\\n<div class=\\\\\\"overflow-x-auto\\\\\\"><table><thead><tr><th>Requirement in the choice</th><th>Where it appears in the original argument</th></tr></thead><tbody><tr><td>An observation is made</td><td>John: celebrities have the biggest smiles in front of the camera</td></tr><tr><td>The observation is used to justify a claim</td><td>John uses the smiles to conclude celebrities are the happiest people alive</td></tr><tr><td>The author treats the observation as insufficient</td><td>David says the smiles do not establish happiness and could be a performance</td></tr><tr><td>The argument concludes the supported claim is false</td><td>David rejects John’s claim and cites depression and substance abuse</td></tr></tbody></table></div>\\\\n<p><st…94 tokens truncated…opics is not enough. The right statements must be doing the right jobs.</p>\\\\n<p>reread the original exchange and choice. Identify the observation, the claim it supports, and David’s response in separate written lines. Compare each line with the matching table.</p>\\";\\nsrc/topics/abstract/components/views/AdvancedChoiceLessons.tsx:13:const MODULE_5_EXPLANATION = \\"<p>Start with the politician’s conclusion: the lower the income-tax rate, the more revenue the tax generates. The politician takes the result at a 100-percent rate and draws a broad relationship between lower rates and higher revenue.</p>\\\\n<p>The economist does not cite a country where a tax cut failed. The economist does not dispute that economic activity would cease at the 100-percent rate. The economist takes the politician’s conclusion to the other end of the range: a rate of zero percent.</p>\\\\n<p>If lowering the rate always raises revenue, a zero-percent rate would produce the maximum. But a tax charged at zero percent collects no income-tax revenue. The economist uses that consequence to challenge the politician’s conclusion.</p>\\\\n<div class=\\\\\\"definition\\\\\\"><p><strong>That is the reasoning move: apply the politician’s rule at its lower limit and show that the resulting conclusion cannot be right.</strong></p></div>\\\\n<h2>Connect that move to the answer’s language</h2>\\\\n<p>Choice C calls the politician’s conclusion a <strong>principle</strong>. Here it is the general relationship the politician claims: lower rate, more revenue. <strong>If applied in the limiting case</strong> refers to applying that relationship at the zero-percent rate. <strong>Leads to an absurdly false conclusion</strong> refers to the claim that a zero-percent income tax would generate maximum revenue.</p>\\\\n<p>Each part names something we can point to. The answer is C.</p>\\\\n<p>Notice how other choices change the action. B describes real results from places that adopted the advice; no such evidence is given. D describes an open attack on the politician’s understanding of economics; the economist instead points to a consequence of the politician’s conclusion. E says a premise is challenged; the response challenges the conclusion drawn from the premise.</p>\\\\n<p>Before selecting a methods label, explain the actual move in ordinary words. Here, “the economist uses a zero-percent tax to show what goes wrong with the politician’s rule” connects the reason to the response. “They disagree about taxes” does not.</p>\\\\n<p>identify the politician’s rule, the case the economist applies it to, and the result that makes the rule untenable. Then match those three parts to C.</p>\\";\\nsrc/topics/abstract/components/views/AdvancedChoiceLessons.tsx:63:    <button type=\\"button\\" disabled={!account.trim()} aria-expanded={revealed} onClick={() => setRevealed(!revealed)} className=\\"brutal-btn bg-brutal-ink text-brutal-paper\\">{revealed ? \\"Hide the explanation\\" : \\"Compare my reasoning with the explanation\\"}</button>\\n"}'}]
